Rough volatility, path-dependent PDEs and weak rates of convergence Thanks: We would like to thank Christian Bayer for introducing us to the weak rate problem and an anonymous referee for bringing Lemma 3.7 to our attention. The authors acknowledge financial support from the EPSRC grant EP/T032146/1.
Ofelia Bonesini, Antoine Jacquier, Alexandre Pannier

Abstract
. In the setting of stochastic Volterra equations, and in particular rough volatility models, we show that conditional expectations are the unique classical solutions to path-dependent PDEs. The latter arise from the functional Itô formula developed by [Viens, F., & Zhang, J. (2019). A martingale approach for fractional Brownian motions and related path dependent PDEs. Ann. Appl. Probab. ]. We then leverage these tools to study weak rates of convergence for discretised stochastic integrals of smooth functions of a Riemann-Liouville fractional Brownian motion with Hurst parameter H ∈ ( 0 , 1 2 ) H\in(0,\frac{1}{2}) . These integrals approximate log-stock prices in rough volatility models. We obtain the optimal weak error rates of order 1 1 if the test function is quadratic and of order ( 3 ​ H + 1 2 ) ∧ 1 (3H+\frac{1}{2})\wedge 1 if the test function is five times differentiable; in particular these conditions are independent of the value of H H .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
60G22, 35K10, 65C20, 91G20, 91G60

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Motivation

Until and unless softwares become capable of handling infinite quantities in finite time, numerical error analysis justifies the application of continuous-time models to discretised real-world applications. In this paper, we consider a time-grid of
(N+1)
equally spaced points
(t_{i}:=\frac{iT}{N})_{i=0}^{N}
and we study the convergence, as
\Delta=\frac{T}{N}
goes to zero, of the Euler approximation

(1.1)

\overline{X}_{t_{0}}=x_{0},\qquad\overline{X}_{t_{i+1}}=\overline{X}_{t_{i}}+\psi(V_{t_{i}})(B_{t_{i+1}}-B_{t_{i}})-\frac{1}{2}\psi(V_{t_{i}})^{2}\Delta,

to the original rough volatility model, where
X
represents the log-price,

(1.2)

X_{t}=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\psi(V_{r})\,\mathrm{d}B_{r}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\psi(V_{r})^{2}\mathrm{d}r,\quad V_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}(t-r)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}\,\mathrm{d}W_{r}.

Here,
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
,
B
and
W
are correlated Brownian motions with the natural filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{T}}
, and
V
is a Gaussian (Volterra) process with known covariance function, which can therefore be exactly sampled at discrete-time points by Cholesky decomposition.
The pivotal parameter is
H
, which controls the Hölder regularity of
V
. When
H=\frac{1}{2}
, one recovers the well-known Markov and semimartingale theories. The singular case
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, supported by empirical data under both historical and pricing measures
[
3
,
7
,
12
,
18
,
19
,
33
,
38
]
, is precisely where both theoretical and numerical analyses go haywire. While
X
remains a semimartingale,
V
fails to be so, and an application of Itô’s formula with
\psi
Lipschitz continuous shows that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert X_{T}-\overline{X}_{T}\right\rvert^{2}\right]\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\mathbb{E}[\left\lvert V_{r}-V_{t_{i}}\right\rvert^{2}]\mathrm{d}r\lesssim\Delta^{2H},

namely that the
strong rate of convergence
is of order
H
. When
H
is close to zero, as is generally agreed upon in the community, a practical implementation would not converge in a reasonable amount of time (dividing the error by
2
requires multiplying
N
by
2^{1/H}
). This shortcoming is confirmed by the lower bound of
[
25
, Theorem 2]
, and in the more general framework of Stochastic Volterra Equations (SVEs) by
[
21
, Theorems 2.3 and 2.4]
,
[
32
, Theorems 2.2 and 2.4]
(rate
H
and
2H
for Euler and Milstein schemes respectively) and
[
2
, Corollary 3.1]
(rate
4H/3
with a multifactor approximation). A more precise analysis was undertaken
in
[
16
, Theorem 2.1]
and
[
27
, Theorems 2.1 and 2.2]
, which provide a Central Limit Theorem for the asymptotic error between an SVE and its Euler discretisation. The rescaling is the strong error rate
N^{H}
.

1.2.
Weak rates

Fortunately, the financial applications we have in mind, such as option pricing, only require to approximate quantities of the form
\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T})]
, for a payoff function
\phi
.
This falls into the realm of the
weak rate of convergence
which is usually much higher than its strong counterpart. Most friendly stochastic integrals, such as Itô’s SDEs where
H=\frac{1}{2}
, exhibit
1
for the former and
\frac{1}{2}
for the latter. One then naturally wonders how the weak error rate evolves in the inhospitable interval
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
. In particular, a positive lower bound is needed to justify the use of simulation schemes.

Unfortunately, the analysis of the weak rate turns out to be an intricate problem, even in relatively simple settings. For
H>\frac{1}{2}
,
[
6
, Theorem 18]
obtained a weak rate of order
H
where the volatility follows a fractional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process. Let us also mention
[
24
]
for the analysis of fractional SDEs with
H>\frac{1}{3}
, where a weak rate of
4H-1
is obtained via rough paths and Malliavin calculus techniques.

Regarding rough volatility models, the rough Donsker theorem of
[
20
, Theorem 2.11]
, with weak rate
H
, was designed in an age when bare convergence was already an achievement, while the quantization method developed in
[
8
]
only applies to VIX derivatives.
Regarding the weak error rate between (
1.1
) and (
1.2
), the following table is, as far as we are aware, an exhaustive literature review of the results obtained so far.

Authors

Weak rate

Assumptions

Bayer, Hall, Tempone

[
5
, Theorem 2.1]

H+\frac{1}{2}

Linear variance (
\psi(x)=x
), bounded payoff (
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{b}^{\lceil\frac{1}{H}\rceil}
)

Bayer, Fukasawa, Nakahara

[
4
, Theorem 1.2]

H+\frac{1}{2}

Linear variance (
\psi(x)=x
),
regular payoff (
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{p}^{2+\lceil\frac{1}{2H}\rceil}
)

Gassiat

[
17
, Theorem 2.1]

(3H+\frac{1}{2})\wedge 1

Linear variance (
\psi(x)=x
),
bounded payoff (
\phi\in\mathcal{C}_{b}^{3+2\lceil\frac{1}{4H}\rceil}
)

or

regular variance (
\psi\in\mathcal{C}_{b}^{2}
), cubic payoff (
\phi(x)=x^{3}
)

Friz, Salkeld, Wagenhofer

[
14
, Theorem 1.1]

(3H+\frac{1}{2})\wedge 1

Regular variance (
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{m}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
), polynomial payoff (
\phi(x)=x^{n},\,n\leq m
)

They give a positive answer to the question of the lower bound: a minimum of
\frac{1}{2}
is achieved in all these papers, even supplemented by an unexpected
3H
(and a logarithmic correction for
H=\frac{1}{6}
) in the last two. On the other hand, all of them rely one way or another on the given structure of the model (the last column) to derive explicit computations.
Friz, Salkeld and Wagenhofer
[
14
]
built on previous ideas by Gassiat
[
17
]
, which is itself in a similar spirit as the duality approach in
[
9
]
, to write down an explicit formula for the moments of the stochastic integral in (
1.2
). In both cases, a precise fractional analysis is necessary to wind up with a sweet
3H+\frac{1}{2}
weak rate. Furthermore, let us mention that the constant obtained in
[
14
]
blows up as
n
goes to infinity, ruling out a polynomial approximation of more general payoffs. The authors also prove the optimality of this rate by providing a counterexample when
\phi
is cubic function
[
14
, Proposition 6.1]
.

This problem is difficult because, here more than elsewhere, standard techniques for diffusions rely on Itô’s formula or on the Markov property, and in particular on PDE methods
[
34
,
35
,
28
]
.
Inspired by the functional Itô formula developed in
[
36
, Theorem 3.10 and 3.17]
and the resulting path-dependent PDEs (PPDEs) studied in
[
37
, Theorem 3.1, 3.2, 3.4]
, we decide to explore a PDE approach analogous to the Markov case. The aim of this paper is then twofold:

(1)

We present the general PPDE theory for SVEs (Section
2.3
) and prove that it applies to rough volatility models (Section
2.4
).

(2)

For the Euler approximation (
1.1
) with no drift and assuming
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{5},\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{3}
with polynomial and exponential growth respectively, we derive the optimal weak error rate of order
(3H+\frac{1}{2})\wedge 1
in Theorem
3.1
.
If
\phi
is quadratic we obtain a weak error of rate one.

Relaxing the requirements on
\phi
and
\psi
is the main achievement of this work compared to former results as it breaks away from the polynomial setting and provides a condition independent of
H
(the constant may blow up as
H\downarrow 0
though).
Although the error analysis is restricted to the case without drift and exactly sampled variance for conciseness, the PDE approach should stretch beyond this setting without difficulty, and even has the potential to extend to larger classes of SVEs and different types of approximation.

1.3.
Path-dependent PDEs

Viens and Zhang
[
36
]
were interested in understanding the path-dependent structure of conditional expectations of functionals of Volterra processes. Let us start by explaining the idea at the core of their paper in a simple setting.
For
0\leq t\leq s\leq T
, the authors discarded the decomposition
V_{s}=V_{t}+[V_{s}-V_{t}]
as
V
does not have a flow or the Markov property. Instead, they promoted the decomposition

V_{s}=\int_{0}^{s}K(s,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}=\underbrace{\int_{0}^{t}K(s,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}}_{\text{\normalsize{$=:\Theta^{t}_{s}$}}}+\underbrace{\int_{t}^{s}K(s,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}}_{\text{\normalsize{$=:I^{t}_{s}$}}},

which is an orthogonal decomposition in the sense that, for
t\leq s
,
\Theta^{t}_{s}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable and
I^{t}_{s}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t}
. In financial terms,
\Theta^{t}_{s}=\mathbb{E}[V_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{t}]
corresponds to the forward variance. Moreover, it turns out that
\Theta^{t}
encodes precisely the path-dependence one needs to express the conditional expectation. Consider (
1.2
) and ignore the drift for clarity, then it holds

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T})|\mathcal{F}_{t}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(X_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}\psi(V_{s})\mathrm{d}B_{s}\right)\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(X_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}\psi(\Theta^{t}_{s}+I^{t}_{s})\mathrm{d}B_{s}\right)\Big|X_{t},\Theta^{t}_{[t,T]}\right]

\displaystyle=u\left(t,X_{t},\Theta^{t}_{[t,T]}\right),

where
u:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\times C([t,T])\to\mathbb{R}
is defined as

(1.3)

u(t,x,\omega):=\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(X_{T})|X_{t}=x,\,\Theta^{t}=\omega\right].

This means that the conditional expectation is only a function of
X_{t}
and
\Theta^{t}_{[t,T]}
, in other words we do not need to take into account the past of
X
and
V
, respectively
X_{[0,t)}
and
V_{[0,t)}
.
We pay the recovery of the Markov property of
(X,\Theta^{t})
(see
[
15
, Proposition 1 and Corollary]
for a proof) by lifting the state space to a path space. Let us mention that such a representation was achieved the same year by
[
13
, Theorem 2.1]
for the rough Heston model using different techniques and that, in the affine framework, this also induces a stochastic PDE representation
[
1
, Equation (3.5)]
,
[
10
, Theorem 5.12]
.

In a more general setting where
\bm{X}
solves an SVE like (
2.1
) and
\bm{\Theta}^{t}
is the appropriate
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable projection, the main result of
[
36
]
is a functional Itô formula
[
36
, Theorem 3.17]
for functions of the concatenated process
\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}:=\bm{X}\mathbbm{1}_{[0,t)}+\bm{\Theta}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}
taking values in
C(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{d})
, see Section
2
.
This involves Fréchet derivatives where the path is only perturbed on
[t,T]
in the direction of the kernel.
When the kernel
K(\cdot,t)
is singular at
t
, hence not continuous on
[t,T]
, the derivative is defined as the limit of those derivatives perturbed by a truncated kernel.

Using BSDE techniques and an intermediary process, in
[
37
, Theorem 3.2]
the authors demonstrated that conditional expectations of the latter are classical solutions to (semilinear parabolic) path-dependent PDEs, as alluded to in
[
36
]
.
As we detail in Remark
2.17
, the Fréchet derivative—morally, because in a different space—generalises Dupire’s vertical derivative
[
11
]
as the path is not frozen and the direction not constant. The Volterra case leads to a different class of PPDEs which do not lie in the scope of previous existence results
[
30
,
31
]
.

We adapt the proof of
[
37
, Theorem 3.2]
to include singular kernels, derive uniqueness of the solution, and show the connection with the original SVE. This confirms that the representation (
1.3
) holds for a broad class of SVEs. The caveat lies in the stringent assumptions made on the road to Itô’s formula: the function
u
needs to be twice Fréchet differentiable with particular regularity conditions. Exploiting the smoothness of the payoff
\phi
and of the volatility function
\psi
, we are however able to verify these for rough volatility models of interest (
1.2
), and thus prove well-posedness of the pricing PPDE (
2.33
).
Outside weak error rates, we believe this opens the gates to a number of PDE applications for rough volatility, such as but not limited to, stochastic control and optimisation, numerical methods
[
23
,
29
]
and regularity of the value function.

1.4.
Method of proof

The idea of our proof consists in expressing the error
\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T})]-\mathbb{E}[\phi(\overline{X}_{T})]
as a telescopic sum of conditional expectations between successive discretisation points as in
[
28
, Section 7.6]
and
[
5
, Section 3.3]
for instance. We apply the functional Itô formula to the Euler approximation between
t_{i}
and
t_{i+1}
and then cancel the time derivative of
u
thanks to the PPDE; this is presented in Proposition
3.5
. The problem boils down to studying local errors of the type

(1.4)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\psi^{2}(V_{t})-\psi^{2}(V_{t_{i}})\right)\phi^{\prime\prime}(X_{T})\right].

Only a sharp analysis can exploit this difference without exhibiting the strong rate. Indeed, the variance process is not a semimartingale thus we cannot apply Itô’s formula again and standard approximations and estimates necessitate to take absolute values, hence invoking the strong rate.
On the other hand, one can show that
\mathbb{E}\big[\psi^{2}(V_{t})-\psi^{2}(V_{t_{i}})\big]\lesssim t^{2H}-t_{i}^{2H}
. This only yields a local weak rate of
2H
, but the passage to the global rate (after integrating over
t
and summing over
i
) achieves a rate of order one (see Lemma
3.8
). This trick allows us to recover this optimal rate for quadratic payoffs, and inspires us to look for ways of disentangling the stochastic factors in (
1.4
).

The crucial tool for expressing the differences in (
1.4
) without taking absolute values—which would only yield the strong rate of convergence,
H
—is found in a combination of the Clark-Ocone formula and integration by parts.
This approach effectively decouples the stochastic terms from the kernels, eliminating the need for crude estimates like those provided by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. However, for some of the terms, applying such a strategy once is not sufficient; multiple applications are required but ultimately succeed in achieving the desired rate.

In a nutshell, this paper is a tale that starts from a numerical analysis problem, then dives into stochastic analysis before bouncing back on PDE theory, adds a salty touch of Malliavin calculus and ends its course with fractional calculus.

1.5.
Organisation of the paper

The rest of the paper is structured as follows:
In Section
2.2
, we introduce the framework and definitions needed to extend the Itô formula to functionals with super-polynomial growth.
In Section
2.3
, the connection with the path-dependent PDE is established, as in the Markovian setting. We apply these results to rough volatility models in Section
2.4
and postpone the proofs to Section
7
.
In Section
3
we state our main result for the weak rates of convergence, whose proof is carried out in details in Sections
5
and
6
.

2.
The functional Itô formula and the path-dependent PDE

2.1.
Notations

We write, for
m,n\in\mathbb{N}
with
m<n
,
[\![m,n]\!]:=\{m,\dots,n\}
and
[\![n]\!]:=[\![1,n]\!]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
We fix a finite time horizon
T>0
and denote the corresponding time interval as
\mathbb{T}:=[0,T]
.
For a couple of topological spaces
\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Z}
, the set
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathcal{Y,Z})
(resp.
D^{0}(\mathcal{Y,Z})
) represents the set of continuous (resp. càdlàg) functions from
\mathcal{Y}
to
\mathcal{Z}
.
We write
f(x)\lesssim g(x)
for two positive functions
f,g
if there exists
c>0
independent of
x
such that
f(x)\leq cg(x)
. We only use this notation when it is clear that the constant
c
is inconsequential. We define
\mathfrak{b}_{p}
to be the BDG constant for any
p>1
.

We consider multi-dimensional stochastic Volterra equations (SVE) given by

(2.1)

\bm{X}_{t}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(t,r,\bm{X}_{r})\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(t,r,\bm{X}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r},

taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with
d\geq 1
.
The coefficients
b:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\sigma:\mathbb{T}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
are Borel-measurable functions,
and
\bm{W}
is an
m
-dimensional Brownian motion on the filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}},\mathbb{P})
satisfying the usual conditions.
For each
t\in\mathbb{T}
, we introduce the key
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable process
(\bm{\Theta}^{t}_{s})_{s\geq t}
:

(2.2)

\bm{\Theta}^{t}_{s}=x+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r}.

We highlight one important property: fixing
s\in\mathbb{T}
and viewing the index
t\in[0,s]
as time,
(\bm{\Theta}^{t}_{s}-\int_{0}^{t}b(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\mathrm{d}r)_{t\in[0,s]}
is a martingale (provided the right integrability conditions).

2.2.
Definitions and the functional Itô formula

We need to introduce a number of notations that lead to the functional Itô formula.
The following is a summary of
[
36
, Section 3.1]
with the small twist that we allow for faster than polynomial growth.
We define the following spaces and distances:

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}:=\mathcal{C}^{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{W}}:=\mathrm{D}^{0}(\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad\mathcal{W}_{t}=\mathcal{C}^{0}([t,T],\mathbb{R}^{d});

\displaystyle\Lambda:=\mathbb{T}\times\mathcal{W},\qquad

\displaystyle\overline{\Lambda}:=\left\{(t,\bm{\omega})\in\mathbb{T}\times\overline{\mathcal{W}}:\bm{\omega}\!\mid_{[t,T]}\in\mathcal{W}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{\omega}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert\bm{\omega}_{t}\right\rvert,\qquad

\displaystyle\bm{d}((t,\bm{\omega}),(t^{\prime},\bm{\omega}^{\prime})):=\left\lvert t-t^{\prime}\right\rvert+\left\lVert\bm{\omega}-\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}.

For two paths
\bm{\omega},\bm{\theta}
on
\mathbb{T}
we define their concatenation at time
t\in\mathbb{T}
as

\bm{\omega}\otimes_{t}\bm{\theta}:=\bm{\omega}\mathbbm{1}_{[0,t)}+\bm{\theta}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}.

Let
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Lambda}):=\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Lambda},\mathbb{R})
denote the set of functions
U:\overline{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{R}
continuous under
\bm{d}
and define the right time derivative

\partial_{t}U(t,\bm{\omega}):=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{U(t+\varepsilon,\bm{\omega})-U(t,\bm{\omega})}{\varepsilon},

for all
(t,\bm{\omega})\in\overline{\Lambda}
, provided the limit exists.
We also recall the spatial Fréchet derivative
\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega})
with respect to
\bm{\omega}
:

(2.3)

U(t,\bm{\omega}+\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]})-U(t,\bm{\omega})=\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\eta\rangle+o(\left\lVert\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}),\quad\text{for all }\eta\in\mathcal{W}.

This is a linear operator on
\mathcal{W}
and, if it exists, it is equal to the Gateaux derivative

(2.4)

\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\eta\rangle=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow 0}\frac{U(t,\bm{\omega}+\varepsilon\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]})-U(t,\bm{\omega})}{\varepsilon}.

We similarly define the second derivative
\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\eta^{(1)},\eta^{(2)})\rangle
.The following assumption ensures well-posedness of (
2.1
).

Assumption 2.1
.

The coefficients
b
and
\sigma
are such that:

(i)

The SVE (
2.1
) has a weak solution
(\bm{X},\bm{W})
;

(ii)

For all
p\geq 1
,
\mathbb{E}[\|\bm{X}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{p}]=\mathbb{E}[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert\bm{X}_{t}\right\rvert^{p}]<\infty
.

This assumption, from
[
36
]
, only requires
\bm{X}
to have finite moments forcing us to take into account only functions of
\bm{X}
with polynomial growth.
We would like to relax this assumption.

Definition 2.2
.

Given (
2.1
),
let
\mathcal{X}:=\{G:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}:\mathbb{E}[G(\|{\bm{X}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{p})]<\infty\text{ for all }p>1\}
.

Example 2.3
.

(1)

If
b
and
\sigma
have linear growth in space then standard arguments show that
\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{X}}\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{p}]
is finite for all
p>1
, so that
\mathcal{X}
includes all functions with at most polynomial growth.

(2)

If
b,\sigma
are independent of
x
then
\bm{X}
is a Gaussian Volterra process, thus
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{p\left\lVert\bm{X}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}}]<\infty
for all
p>1

[
22
, Lemma 6.13]
and
\mathcal{X}
includes all functions of at most exponential growth.

(3)

The model we have in mind for relaxing the polynomial growth assumption of
[
36
]
is a rough volatility model where the second component (the stochastic process driving the volatility) is a Gaussian Volterra process and the first (the log-price) is a non-Gaussian stochastic integral. The latter has finite moments of all orders while the former has finite exponential moments (by item (2) of this list). Hence in this framework the set
\mathcal{X}
includes all functions with at most polynomial growth in the first component and exponential growth in the second, that is for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

G(x)\leq C\left(1+|x^{(1)}|+\exp\{x^{(2)}\}\right),\quad\text{for some }C>0,

A thorough examination of the proofs in
[
36
, Theorems 3.10 and 3.17]
confirms that the functional Itô formula still stands with the new growth definitions that follow.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
U\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Lambda})
such that
\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U
exists for all
(t,\bm{\omega})\in\overline{\Lambda}
.
For
G\in\mathcal{X}
, we say that
\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U
has
G
-growth if

\left\lvert\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\eta\rangle\right\rvert\lesssim G(\left\lVert\bm{\omega}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}})\left\lVert\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}},\qquad\text{ for all }\eta\in\mathcal{W},\text{ for all }t\in\mathbb{T},

and similarly for all

\eta^{(1)},\eta^{(2)}\in\mathcal{W}

and all
t\in\mathbb{T}
,

\left\lvert\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\eta^{(1)},\eta^{(2)})\rangle\right\rvert\lesssim G(\left\lVert\bm{\omega}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}})\left\lVert\eta^{(1)}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lVert\eta^{(2)}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}.

Definition 2.5
.

We say
U\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2}(\overline{\Lambda})\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Lambda})
if
\partial_{t}U,\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U,\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U
exist and are continuous on
\overline{\Lambda}
.
Moreover,
U\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+}(\overline{\Lambda})\subset\mathcal{C}^{1,2}(\overline{\Lambda})
if there exists
G\in\mathcal{X}
such that all derivatives of
U
have
G
-growth.

Assumption 2.6
.

For
\varphi=b,\sigma
, for every
s\in(t,T]
, with
t\in\mathbb{T}
,
\partial_{t}\varphi(s,t,\cdot)
exists and there exist
G\in\mathcal{X}
and
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
such that, for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\left\lvert\varphi(s,t,x)\right\rvert\lesssim G(x)(s-t)^{H-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\left\lvert\partial_{t}\varphi(s,t,x)\right\rvert\lesssim G(x)(s-t)^{H-\frac{3}{2}}.

Definition 2.7
.

We say that
U\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+,\alpha}(\Lambda)
with
\alpha\in(0,1)
if there exist a continuous extension of
U
in
\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+}(\overline{\Lambda})
,
a growth function
G\in\mathcal{X}
,
a modulus of continuity function
\varrho\leq G
such that for any
0\leq t<T,\,0<\delta\leq T-t
,
\eta,\eta_{1},\eta_{2}\in\mathcal{W}
with supports contained in
[t,t+\delta]
,

(i)

for any
\bm{\omega}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\bm{\omega}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\in\mathcal{W}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\eta\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert G(\bm{\omega})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lVert\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\delta^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert G(\bm{\omega})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lVert\eta_{1}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lVert\eta_{2}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\delta^{2\alpha};

(ii)

for any other
\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}
such that
\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\in\mathcal{W}_{t}
,

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}(U(t,\bm{\omega})-U(t,\bm{\omega}^{\prime})),\eta\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big[\left\lVert G(\bm{\omega})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}+\left\lVert G(\bm{\omega}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\big]\left\lVert\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\varrho(\left\lVert\bm{\omega}-\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}})\delta^{\alpha},

\displaystyle\left\lvert\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}(U(t,\bm{\omega})-U(t,\bm{\omega}^{\prime})),(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\rangle\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\big[\left\lVert G(\bm{\omega})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}+\left\lVert G(\bm{\omega}^{\prime})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\big]\left\lVert\eta_{1}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\left\lVert\eta_{2}\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}\varrho(\left\lVert\bm{\omega}-\bm{\omega}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}})\delta^{2\alpha};

(iii)

for any
\bm{\omega}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}
,
\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\eta\rangle
and
\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\eta_{1},\eta_{2})\rangle
are continuous in
t
.

Remark 2.8
.

In
[
36
, Definition 3.4]
, the modulus of continuity is assumed bounded, which we relax by bounding the growth of
\varphi(s,t,\cdot)
by
G
, which controls the growth of
\varrho
.

Remark 2.9
.

The time continuity of item (iii) in the list is not necessary to apply Itô’s formula but we need it in the proof of Proposition
2.13
.

We intend to deal with singular coefficients that satisfy Assumption
2.6
but may not be continuous on the diagonal. Hence, for
\varphi=b,\sigma
and
\delta>0
, introduce the truncated functions

\varphi^{\delta}(s,t,x):=\varphi(s\lor(t+\delta);t,x),

and for
u\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+,\alpha}
the spatial derivatives

(2.5)

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\varphi^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\varphi^{\delta,t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle,\qquad\text{for }\varphi\in\{b,\sigma\},

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}}\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}})\rangle

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\delta\downarrow 0}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\sigma^{\delta,t,\bm{\omega}}\sigma^{\delta,t,\bm{\omega}})\rangle,

where
\varphi^{t,\bm{\omega}}(s):=\varphi(s;t,\bm{\omega}_{t})
.
The proof of the functional Itô formula from
[
36
, Theorem 3.17]
remains valid in the setting of Assumption
2.6
.

Theorem 2.10
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.6
, suppose
U\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+,\alpha}
, with
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}-H
,
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
Then the spatial derivatives (
2.5
) exist and the following functional Itô formula holds:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}U(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t})=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{t}U(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t})\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}),(\sigma^{t,\bm{X}},\sigma^{t,\bm{X}})\rangle\mathrm{d}t

(2.6)

\displaystyle+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}),b^{t,\bm{X}}\rangle\mathrm{d}t+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}),\sigma^{t,\bm{X}}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{t}.

Remark 2.11
.

One can show that if
\bm{\omega}
is
\mathbb{R}^{d}
-valued, then for
\eta
(living in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
as well),

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\eta\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^{d}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}_{i}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\eta_{i}\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\eta,\eta)\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}_{i}\bm{\omega}_{j}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\eta_{i},\eta_{j})\rangle.

Furthermore, if
\bm{\eta}
is matrix valued, say in
\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}
, then

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}u(t,\bm{\omega}),\bm{\eta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{d}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}_{i}}u(t,\bm{\omega}),\bm{\eta}_{i,j}\rangle\right)_{j=1}^{m},

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}u(t,\bm{\omega}),(\bm{\eta},\bm{\eta})\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}_{i}\bm{\omega}_{j}}u(t,\bm{\omega}),(\bm{\eta}_{j,k},\bm{\eta}_{i,k})\rangle.

In finite dimensions, these correspond to the gradient and trace of the Hessian, respectively.

2.3.
A path-dependent PDE

As in the Markovian case, this Itô formula leads to natural connections with PDEs. In this section, we rigorously prove that conditional expectations (equivalently, solutions to a BSDE) are solutions to path-dependent PDEs. This was predicted by
[
36
, Section 4.2]
where they also showed the reverse implication, known as Feynman-Kac formula.
The crucial aspect is to show time differentiability, for which we follow
[
37
, Theorem 3.2]
, where a similar result is presented for regular kernels. This requires to introduce new variables and notations.
Define the forward-backward SVE for
\bm{\omega}\in\mathcal{W}
and all
0\leq t\leq s\leq T
:

(2.7)

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{s}^{t,\bm{\omega}}

\displaystyle=\bm{\omega}_{s}+\int_{t}^{s}b(s,r,\bm{X}_{r}^{t,\bm{\omega}})\mathrm{d}r+\int_{t}^{s}\sigma(s,r,\bm{X}_{r}^{t,\bm{\omega}})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r}

(2.8)

\displaystyle\bm{Y}_{s}^{t,\bm{\omega}}

\displaystyle=\Phi(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{\cdot})+\int_{s}^{T}f(r,\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r},\bm{Y}_{r}^{t,\bm{\omega}},\bm{Z}_{r}^{t,\bm{\omega}})\mathrm{d}r-\int_{s}^{T}\bm{Z}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r}.

The forward process
\bm{X}
lives in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and the backward one
\bm{Y}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
with
d,d^{\prime}\in\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover
\Phi
maps
\mathcal{W}
to
\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
and
f:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}\times d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d^{\prime}}
is measurable in all variables.
Under the following assumptions, the backward SDE has a unique square integrable solution
[
39
, Theorem 4.3.1]
.

Assumption 2.12
.

(i)

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}})^{2}]<\infty
, for all
\bm{\omega}\in\mathcal{W}
and all
0\leq t\leq s\leq T
;

(ii)

f
is uniformly Lipschitz continuous in
(y,z)
and continuous in
t
;

(iii)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert f(r,\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r},0,0)\right\rvert\mathrm{d}r\right)^{2}\right]
is finite.

Condition (iii) is satisfied if, for instance, there exist
G\in\mathcal{X}
and
g\in L^{1}(\mathbb{T})
such that
f(r,\bm{\omega},0,0)\leq g(r)\sqrt{\left\lVert G(\bm{\omega})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}}
.
We now show that

(2.9)

\displaystyle U(t,\bm{\omega}):=\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{t}

satisfies a semi-linear path-dependent PDE.

Proposition 2.13
.

Let Assumptions
2.1
,
2.6
and
2.12
hold.
Moreover, assume the SVE (
2.7
) has a unique strong solution and
U\in\mathcal{C}^{0,2}_{+,\alpha}
, with
\alpha>\frac{1}{2}-H
and
H\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
.
Then the time derivative of
U
exists and
U
is a classical solution to the path-dependent PDE

(2.10)

\partial_{t}U(t,\bm{\omega})+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}},\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}})\rangle+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),b^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle+f\big(t,\bm{\omega},U(t,\bm{\omega}),\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle\big)=0,

with boundary condition
U(T,\bm{\omega})=\Phi(\bm{\omega})
.

Proof.

We follow the proof of
[
37
, Theorem 3.2]
but drop one argument (the first one in
U
) which makes it slightly simpler.

Step 1.
For any
0\leq t\leq r\leq s\leq T
and any
\bm{\omega}\in\mathcal{W}
, define

(2.11)

\widetilde{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r,s}:=\bm{\omega}_{s}+\int_{t}^{r}b(s,r^{\prime},\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}+\int_{t}^{r}\sigma(s,r^{\prime},\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r^{\prime}})\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r^{\prime}}.

Hence, by strong uniqueness of solutions for (
2.7
), we have

\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{s}=\bm{X}_{s}^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}},\qquad\text{where }\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r^{\prime}}:=\big(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}\otimes_{r}\widetilde{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r}\big)_{r^{\prime}}=\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{r^{\prime}<r}+\widetilde{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r,r^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{r^{\prime}\geq r}.

By uniqueness of the solution to the BSDE, then
\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r}=\bm{Y}^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}}_{r}=U(r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r})
.

Step 2.
Fix
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\delta=T/n
,
t_{i}=i\delta
for all
i\leq n
. For
r\in(t_{i},t_{i+1}]
, denote

\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}:=U(t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}),\qquad\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}:=\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}),\sigma^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}}\rangle.

Thanks to our assumptions we apply the functional Itô formula to
U(t_{i+1},\cdot)
on
(t_{i},t_{i+1}]
:

(2.12)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}=\bigg[\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U(t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}),(\sigma^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}},\sigma^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}})\rangle+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}),b^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}}\rangle\bigg]\mathrm{d}r+\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r},\qquad

where we used the fact that in (
2.11
),
\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r^{\prime}}=\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r^{\prime}}
for
r^{\prime}\in[t,r)
.
Now define
\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}:=\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}-\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r}
and
\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}:=\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}-\bm{Z}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r}
which sastify a new BSDE

(2.13)

\mathrm{d}\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}=\widehat{f}\left(r,t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r},\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}-\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{r},\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}-\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}\right)\mathrm{d}r+\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r},

where, for
r\in(t_{i},t_{i+1}]
,

\widehat{f}(r,t_{i+1},x,y,z):=\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}^{2}U(t_{i+1},x),(\sigma^{r,x},\sigma^{r,x})\rangle+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t_{i+1},x),b^{r,x}\rangle+f(r,x,y,z).

Since
\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{t_{i+1}}=0
, we use standard BSDE estimates
[
39
, Theorem 4.2.1]
, the uniform Lipschitz continuity of
(y,z)\mapsto f(r,x,y,z)
and Jensen’s inequality to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{t_{i}<r\leq t_{i+1}}\left\lvert\Delta\bm{Y}_{r}^{n}\right\rvert^{2}+\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left\lvert\Delta\bm{Z}_{r}^{n}\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\widehat{f}(r,t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r},\bm{Y}^{n}_{r},\bm{Z}^{n}_{r})\mathrm{d}r\right)^{2}\right]

(2.14)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(r,t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r},0,0)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}\right\rvert^{2}+|\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right].

The same estimates, this time applied to the BSDE (
2.12
), give

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{0\leq r\leq T}

\displaystyle\left\lvert\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}\right\rvert^{2}+\int_{0}^{T}|\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\Big]

(2.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert\Phi(\bm{X}^{s,\bm{\omega}})\right\rvert^{2}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{T}\widehat{f}\left(r,\frac{\lceil rN\rceil}{N},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r},0,0\right)-f(r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r},0,0)\mathrm{d}r\right)^{2}\right],

which is finite by Assumption
2.12
(i) and
G
-growth of the pathwise derivatives.

The same
G
-growth of the pathwise derivatives yields

(2.16)

\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left\lvert\widehat{f}(r,t_{i+1},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r},0,0)\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]<\infty.

We combine the previous estimates in (
2.3
), (
2.3
) and (
2.16
) to conclude this step:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left\lvert\bm{Z}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r}-\big\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{r,\bm{\omega},r}),\sigma^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}}\big\rangle\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left\lvert\Delta\bm{Z}_{r}^{n}\right\rvert^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right]

\displaystyle\leq\delta\,\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{T}\left\lvert\widehat{f}\left(r,\frac{\lceil rN\rceil}{N},\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}_{r},0,0\right)\right\rvert^{2}+\left\lvert\bm{Y}^{n}_{r}\right\rvert^{2}+|\bm{Z}^{n}_{r}|^{2}\mathrm{d}r\right],

which goes to zero as
\delta\downarrow 0
, and therefore implies
\bm{Z}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{r}=\Big\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{r,\bm{\omega},r}),\sigma^{r,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},r}}\Big\rangle
.

Step 3.
In particular, setting
r=t
in (
2.13
) we have
\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},t}=\bm{\omega}
and

\mathrm{d}\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{t}=\widehat{f}(t,t_{i+1},\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{s},\bm{Z}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{t})\mathrm{d}t+\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{t}.

Let
t=t_{i}
, hence
t_{i+1}=t+\delta
, and recall
\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{t_{i+1}}=0
, therefore we have

\displaystyle U(t+\delta,\bm{\omega})-U(t,\bm{\omega})

\displaystyle=-(\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{t_{i+1}}-\Delta\bm{Y}^{n}_{t_{i}})

(2.17)

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\widehat{f}(v,v,\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{s,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{n}_{v})\mathrm{d}v\right]+R(\delta),

where we took expectations because the left-hand-side is deterministic and where

\displaystyle R(\delta):=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}

\displaystyle\Big\{\widehat{f}(v,v,\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{s,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{n}_{v})-\widehat{f}(v,v,\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{s,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{n}_{v}+\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{v})\Big\}

\displaystyle+\Big\{\widehat{f}(v,v,\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{s,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{t,x}_{v})-\widehat{f}(v,t_{i+1},\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{t,x}_{v})\Big\}\mathrm{d}v\bigg].

We first apply Jensen’s inequality and
(a+b)^{2}\leq 2(a^{2}+b^{2})
, then Lipschitz continuity in
z
and estimate (
2.3
) for the first difference, and the regularity of
U
and
f
for the second (Definition
2.7
(iii) as
U\in\mathcal{C}^{0,2}_{+,\alpha}(\Lambda)
and Assumption
2.12
):

\displaystyle\left\lvert R(\delta)\right\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\Big\{\widehat{f}(v,v,\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{n}_{v})-\widehat{f}(v,v,\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{n}_{v}+\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{v})\Big\}^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big\{\widehat{f}(v,v,\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{n}_{v}+\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{v})-\widehat{f}(v,t_{i+1},\bm{\omega}_{t},\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{v},\bm{Z}^{n}_{v}+\Delta\bm{Z}^{n}_{v})\Big\}^{2}\mathrm{d}v\bigg]

\displaystyle=o(\delta^{2}).

Hence, dividing (
2.3
) by
\delta
and taking
\delta
to zero, the time derivative exists and is equal to

-\partial_{t}U(t,\bm{\omega})=\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}^{2}U(t,\bm{\omega}),(\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}},\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}})\rangle+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),b^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle+f\big(t,\bm{\omega}_{t},U(t,\bm{\omega}),\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U(t,\bm{\omega}),\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle\big).

Finally,
U(T,x)=\bm{Y}_{T}^{T,x}=\Phi(\bm{X}^{T,\bm{\omega}})=\Phi(\bm{\omega})
, and thus
U
is a classical solution to the PPDE.
∎

Proposition 2.14
.

Under the same assumptions as in Proposition
2.13
, the PPDE (
2.10
) with its boundary condition has a unique
\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+,\alpha}
solution.

Proof.

This is inspired by the proof of
[
30
, Theorem 4.1]
.
Given the assumptions, Proposition
2.13
guarantees the existence of a solution
u\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+,\alpha}
to the PPDE.
Apply Itô’s formula (
2.6
) to
u(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s})

and, after cancelling the terms from the PPDE, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s})=

\displaystyle-f\left(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}_{s},u(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}),\left\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}u(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}),\sigma^{s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}}\right\rangle\right)\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle+\left\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}u(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}),\sigma^{s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}}\right\rangle\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{s},

where we recall that
\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}_{s}=\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{s}
.
Combined with the boundary condition and the fact that the BSDE in (
2.8
) has a unique solution by Assumption
2.12
, this implies

(\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{s},\bm{\bm{Z}}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{s})=\big(u(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}),\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}u(s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}),\sigma^{s,\widehat{\bm{X}}^{t,\bm{\omega},s}}\rangle\big)

is the unique solution to the BSDE (
2.8
).
In particular,
\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{\omega}}_{t}=u(t,\bm{\omega})
, concluding the proof.
∎

Remark 2.15
.

In financial applications, we are interested in evaluating conditional expectations of the type
\mathbb{E}[\Phi(S)|\mathcal{F}_{t}]

where the asset price
S
is defined explicitly. However in the general Volterra case (where the coefficients are state-dependent) it is not trivial to express it as a function of
(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t})
. With this in mind we observe that, for
0\leq t\leq s\leq T
,

(2.18)

\displaystyle\bm{X}_{s}

\displaystyle=\bm{X}_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\big[b(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\mathrm{d}r+\sigma(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r}\big]+\int_{t}^{s}\big[b(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\mathrm{d}r+\sigma(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r}\big]

(2.19)

\displaystyle=\bm{\Theta}^{t}_{s}+\int_{t}^{s}\big[b(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\mathrm{d}r+\sigma(s,r,\bm{X}_{r})\,\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{r}\big].

Hence, by the strong uniqueness of the solutions to the SVEs (
2.1
) and (
2.7
),

\bm{X}=\bm{X}^{t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}}
almost surely on
\mathbb{T}
.

With
\bm{Y}
being the solution of the BSDE (
2.8
) with
f\equiv 0
,
and for any
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable random variable
\xi\in L^{2}(\Omega;\mathcal{W})
, equations (
2.8
) and (
2.9
) entail

(2.20)

U(t,\xi)=\bm{Y}^{t,\xi}_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}^{t,\xi})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{t}].

This yields in particular

(2.21)

\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\bm{X})|\mathcal{F}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\Phi\left(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}}\right)|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\bm{Y}^{t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}}_{t}=U(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}).

Remark 2.16
.

The results of this section do not go through without the assumption of strong uniqueness of the solution to the SVE. This acts as a further impetus for resolving this open problem in the rough Heston model.

Remark 2.17
.

The general setting simplifies when applied to semimartingales and/or payoffs that depend only on the terminal value.
We recall the function
U:\overline{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{R}
given by
U(t,\bm{\omega})=\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}})]
.

(1)

If
K
is a constant, i.e.
\bm{X}
is a Markov process and a semimartingale, then
\bm{\omega}\mapsto U(t,\bm{\omega})
is in fact measurable with respect to
\sigma(\{\bm{\omega}\lvert_{[0,t]}:\bm{\omega}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}\})
, hence there exists

u_{1}:\Big\{(t,\bm{\omega}_{1})\in\mathbb{T}\times\big(\mathrm{D}^{0}([0,t])\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,t)\big)\Big\}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\text{such that}\;u_{1}(t,\bm{\omega}\lvert_{[0,t]})=U(t,\bm{\omega}).

The perturbation then becomes
U(t,\bm{\omega}+\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]})=u_{1}(t,\bm{\omega}\lvert_{[0,t]}+\eta_{t}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\}})
, which consists in perturbing the path only at the end point, akin to Dupire’s vertical derivative
[
11
]
. The conditional expectation (
2.21
) reads
\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\bm{X})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{t}]=u_{1}(t,\bm{X}_{[0,t]})
, which is consistent with the observation that
\bm{\Theta}^{t}_{s}=X_{t}
for all
s\geq t
and which entails that the concatenated path
\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t}
is frozen after
t
. This setting is reminiscent of the functional Itô calculus developed by Dupire
[
11
]
with the aim of pricing path-dependent options on Markovian underlyings. Dupire considers paths stopped after
t
; they are thus of different
lengths
which induce a different state space and a different time derivative. The applications, however, are precisely those encompassed by the map
u_{1}
.

(2)

If
K
is not constant but
\phi
only acts on the terminal time, then
\bm{\omega}\mapsto U(t,\bm{\omega})
is now measurable with respect to
\sigma(\{\bm{\omega}\lvert_{[t,T]}:\bm{\omega}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}\})
, hence there exists

u_{2}:\Big\{(t,\bm{\omega})\in\mathbb{T}\times\big(\mathrm{D}^{0}([t,T])\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}(t,T]\big)\Big\}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\text{such that}\;u_{2}(t,\bm{\omega}\lvert_{[t,T]})=U(t,\bm{\omega}).

The perturbation remains
U(t,\bm{\omega}+\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]})=u_{2}(t,\bm{\omega}\lvert_{[t,T]}+\eta\lvert_{[t,T]})
, and the conditional expectation is
\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{T})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{t}]=u_{2}(t,\bm{\Theta}^{t})
.

(3)

If
K
is constant and
\phi
only acts on the terminal time, then
\bm{\omega}\mapsto U(t,\bm{\omega})
is measurable with respect to
\sigma(\{\bm{\omega}_{t}:\bm{\omega}\in\overline{\mathcal{W}}\})
, hence there exists

u_{3}:\Big\{(t,x)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\Big\}\to\mathbb{R},\quad\text{such that}\;u_{3}(t,\bm{\omega}_{t})=U(t,\bm{\omega}).

The perturbation is
U(t,\bm{\omega}+\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]})=u_{3}(t,\bm{\omega}_{t}+\eta_{t})
. This corresponds to the classical problem of pricing vanilla options in a semimartingale model, with the option price being equal to the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bm{X}_{T})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{t}]=u_{3}(t,\bm{X}_{t})
.

2.4.
Application to rough volatility

We consider the following rough volatility model:

(2.22)

X_{t}:=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\psi(r,V_{r})\mathrm{d}B_{r}+\zeta\int_{0}^{t}\psi(r,V_{r})^{2}\mathrm{d}r,\qquad V_{t}:=\int_{0}^{t}K(t,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r},

with
x_{0},\zeta\in\mathbb{R}
,
B=\overline{\rho}\overline{W}+\rho W
,
W
and
\overline{W}
independent Brownian motions,
\rho\in[-1,1]
and
\overline{\rho}:=\sqrt{1-\rho^{2}}
. The kernel
K:\mathbb{T}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
can be singular,
V
is a Gaussian (Volterra) process and thus has finite exponential moments. We are chiefly interested in the Riemann-Liouville case
K(t,s)=(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}
.
The two-dimensional
\bm{X}=(X,V)^{\top}
is a particular case of SVE (
2.1
) with
d=m=2
and

(2.23)

\displaystyle b(t,r,x_{1},x_{2})=\begin{bmatrix}\zeta\psi(r,x_{2})^{2}\\
0\end{bmatrix},\qquad\quad\sigma(t,r,x_{1},x_{2})=\begin{bmatrix}\bar{\rho}\psi(r,x_{2})&\rho\psi(r,x_{2})\\
0&K(t,r)\end{bmatrix}.

In financial models,
X
is the log of an exponential martingale and thus
\zeta=-\frac{1}{2}
while, for simplicity, previous papers studying weak error rates had set
\zeta=0
. In this section we check all the conditions of Proposition
2.13
and derive the pricing PPDE for the rough volatility model (
2.22
). In particular, this gives a rigorous justification to
[
36
, Remark 5.2]
.

Denote with
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}}
the filtration generated by
(\overline{W},W)
. We note that in this case

(2.24)

\displaystyle\Theta_{s}^{t}=\int_{0}^{t}K(s,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}=\mathbb{E}[V_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{t}],\quad s\geq t.

As a special case of (
2.2
), this process arises from the decomposition of
V
as

V_{s}=\int_{0}^{s}K(s,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}=\int_{0}^{t}K(s,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}+\int_{t}^{s}K(s,r)\mathrm{d}W_{r}=:\Theta_{s}^{t}+I_{s}^{t},\quad\text{for all }0\leq t\leq s\leq T.

This decomposition is orthogonal in the sense that
I_{s}^{t}
is independent from
\mathcal{F}_{t}
whereas
\Theta_{s}^{t}=\mathbb{E}[V_{s}|\mathcal{F}_{t}]
. In particular,
\Theta_{t}^{t}=V_{t}
and for
s\in\mathbb{T}
fixed,
\Theta_{s}^{\cdot}=(\Theta_{s}^{t})_{t\in[0,s]}
is a martingale.

We are interested in representations of the value function
U:\overline{\Lambda}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
U(t,\bm{\omega})=\mathbb{E}[\Phi(\bm{X}^{t,\bm{\omega}})]
, which fits in the framework of the previous subsection with
d=2
. As we saw in the introduction, see (
1.3
), the semimartingality of
X
and the state-dependent payoff imply that the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{t}]
is only a function of
(t,X_{t},\Theta^{t})
. Applying items (2) and (3) of Remark
2.17
sequentially, one shows that there exists

(2.25)

u:\Big\{(t,x,\omega)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\times\big(\mathrm{D}^{0}([t,T])\cap\mathcal{C}^{0}(t,T]\big)\Big\}\to\mathbb{R},\text{ such that }u\big(t,\omega^{(1)}_{t},\omega^{(2)}\lvert_{[t,T]}\big)=U(t,\bm{\omega}).

We thus recover the relation already derived in the introduction

u(t,X_{t},\Theta^{t}_{[t,T]})=\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T})|\mathcal{F}_{t}].

The perturbation in the first component is
U(t,\bm{\omega}+\eta\mathbbm{1}_{[t,T]})=u(t,\omega_{t}^{(1)}+\eta^{(1)}_{t},\omega^{(2)}\lvert_{[t,T]})
and thus corresponds to a standard derivative (right derivative if
\eta^{(1)}_{t}>0
), while the derivative in the second component is the Fréchet derivative defined in (
2.3
).
Analogously to
\Lambda
and
\overline{\Lambda}
, we name the space of interest on which it acts

\Gamma:=\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{W},\qquad\overline{\Gamma}:=\big\{(t,x,\omega)=\left(t,(x,\omega)\right)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\times\overline{\mathcal{W}}:\omega|_{[t,T]}\in\mathcal{W}_{t}\big\},

where
\mathcal{W},\mathcal{W}_{t},\overline{\mathcal{W}}
are defined as in Section
2.2
, with
d=1
. The corresponding state space, analogue to Definition
2.7
, is denoted
\mathcal{C}^{1,2,2}_{+,\alpha}(\Gamma)
.

Definition 2.18
.

(i)

We say that
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{m}_{{\rm poly}}(\mathbb{R})
with
m\in\mathbb{N}
if there exists
\kappa>0
such that

\max_{j\leq m}\left\lvert f^{(j)}(x)\right\rvert\lesssim 1+|x|^{\kappa}

for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

(ii)

We say that
g:\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{0,n}_{{\rm exp}}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
with
n\in\mathbb{N}
if there exists
\kappa>0
such that
\max_{j\leq n}\left\lvert\partial_{x}^{(j)}g(t,x)\right\rvert\lesssim 1+\mathrm{e}^{\kappa(x+t)}

for all
(t,x)\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
.

Assumption 2.19
.

(i)

The payoff function
\phi
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{3}_{{\rm poly}}(\mathbb{R})
with constant
{\kappa_{\phi}}>0
;

(ii)

The volatility function
\psi
belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{0,3}_{{\rm exp}}(\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R})
with constant
{\kappa_{\psi}}>0
. Its first and second derivative in space are denoted by
\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime\prime}
, respectively.

(iii)

The kernel
K
, similarly to Assumption
2.6
, satisfies for all
s<t

\left\lvert K(t,s)\right\rvert\lesssim(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}},\quad\left\lvert\partial_{t}K(t,s)\right\rvert\lesssim(t-s)^{H-\frac{3}{2}}.

Remark 2.20
.

We are limited to polynomial growth for
\phi
because it is an open problem whether
\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{pX_{T}}]<\infty
for most values of
(p,\zeta)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

This assumption paves the way for the following lemma, proved in Section
7.1
.

Lemma 2.21
.

Let Assumption
2.19
(ii) hold.
For all
(t,x,\omega)\in\overline{\Lambda}
and
s\in[t,T]
, define

(2.26)

V^{t,\omega}_{s}:=\omega_{s}+I^{t}_{s},\quad\text{and}\quad X_{T}^{t,x,\omega}:=x+\int_{t}^{T}\psi(s,V^{t,\omega}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}\psi(s,V^{t,\omega}_{s})^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

Then, for all
p\geq 1
,

(2.27)

\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{p\left\lVert V\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}}]<\infty,\quad\sup_{s\in[t,T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{pV^{t,\omega}_{s}}\right]\lesssim\mathrm{e}^{p\omega_{t}},\quad\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lVert X^{t,x,\omega}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}^{p}\right]\lesssim\left\lvert x\right\rvert^{p}+\mathrm{e}^{2{\kappa_{\psi}}p\left\lVert\omega\right\rVert_{\mathbb{T}}}.

This lemma characterises functions belonging to
\mathcal{X}
in the rough volatility framework. Indeed, any function
G:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}

G(x,v)\lesssim 1+|x|^{\ell}+\mathrm{e}^{\ell v}
for some
\ell>0
satisfies

(2.28)

\mathbb{E}\left[\|G(X,V)\|_{\mathbb{T}}\right]<\infty.

Such a function thus belongs to the set
\mathcal{X}
corresponding to the system (
2.22
). These estimates further lead to growth estimates of the pathwise derivatives.
In terms of notations, we write
\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}
for the expectation conditioned on
X_{t}=x,\,\Theta^{t}=\omega
. In other words, it corresponds to the expectation with
X^{t,x,\omega}
and
V^{t,\omega}
, as in the previous lemma.
The following proposition, proved in Section
7.2
, gives an explicit representation of the derivatives of
u
.

Proposition 2.22
.

Let Assumption
2.19
hold.
The function
(x,\omega)\mapsto u(t,x,\omega)

belongs to
\mathcal{C}^{2,2}_{+}
and, for all
(t,x,\omega)\in\overline{\Lambda}
,
\eta
,
\eta^{(1)},\eta^{(2)}\in\mathcal{W}_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{x}u(t,x,\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}[\phi^{\prime}(X_{T})],\qquad\partial_{xx}u(t,x,\omega)=\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}[\phi^{\prime\prime}(X_{T})],

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\omega}u(t,x,\omega),\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}\left[\phi^{\prime}(X_{T})\left\{\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta_{s}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}(\psi^{2})^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}\right],

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}u)(t,x,\omega),\eta\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}\left[\phi^{\prime\prime}(X_{T})\left\{\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta_{s}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}(\psi^{2})^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}\right],

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\omega\omega}u(t,x,\omega),(\eta^{(1)},\eta^{(2)})\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}\bigg[\phi^{\prime\prime}(X_{T})\left\{\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta^{(1)}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}(\psi^{2})^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta^{(1)}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\cdot\left\{\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta^{(2)}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}(\psi^{2})^{\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta^{(2)}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}

\displaystyle+\phi^{\prime}(X_{T})\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta^{(1)}_{s}\eta^{(2)}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}(\psi^{2})^{\prime\prime}(s,V_{s})\eta^{(1)}_{s}\eta^{(2)}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\bigg].

The passage to singular kernels/directions requires some Malliavin calculus, which we briefly recall.
Adopting notations and definitions from
[
26
, Section 1.2]
, we denote by
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
the Malliavin derivative operator with respect to
\bm{W}
and by
\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
its domain of application in
L^{2}(\Omega)
. For
F\in\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}F=(\mathrm{D}^{W}F,\mathrm{D}^{\overline{W}}F)^{\top}=:(\mathrm{D}F,\overline{\mathrm{D}}F)^{\top}
.
The (conditional) Malliavin integration by parts formula plays a crucial role

(2.29)

\mathbb{E}\left[F\int_{t}^{T}\langle h_{s},\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{s}\rangle\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\langle\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{s}F,h_{s}\rangle\mathrm{d}s\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right],

for any
F\in\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
and
h\in L^{2}(\Omega\times\mathbb{T},\mathbb{R}^{2})
, and with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
the inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(not to be confused with the pathwise derivative).
In particular, we use extensively the following application with
h
a real-valued process and
\bm{\rho}=(\rho,\overline{\rho})

(2.30)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[F\int_{t}^{T}h_{s}\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\langle\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{s}F,\bm{\rho}\rangle h_{s}\mathrm{d}s\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right].

We now state the pathwise derivative with respect to the singular kernel,
with proof postponed to Section
7.3
.
This representation resorts to the integration by parts (
2.30
)
since the stochastic integral
\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime\prime}(s,V_{s})K(s,t)^{2}\mathrm{d}B_{s}
is ill-defined in general.

Proposition 2.23
.

Under Assumption
2.19
, for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
, the conclusions of Proposition
2.22
still hold when the directions
\eta,\eta^{(1)},\eta^{(2)}\in\mathcal{W}_{t}
are replaced by the singular kernel
K^{t}:=K(\cdot,t)
, where
K
satisfies Assumption
2.19
(iii), with the modification

(2.31)

\displaystyle\qquad\langle\partial_{\omega\omega}u(t,x,\omega),(K^{t},K^{t})\rangle

(2.32)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}\left[\phi^{\prime\prime}(X_{T})\left\{\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(s,V_{s})K(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}(\psi^{2})^{\prime}(s,V_{s})K(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}s\right\}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}_{t,x,\omega}\left[\int_{t}^{T}\langle\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{s}\phi^{\prime}(X_{T}),\bm{\rho}\rangle\psi^{\prime\prime}(s,V_{s})K(s,t)^{2}\mathrm{d}s+\zeta\int_{t}^{T}(\psi^{2})^{\prime\prime}(s,V_{s})K(s,t)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\right].

The following proposition verifies that the derivatives satisfy the proper regularity conditions of Definition
2.7
and is proved in Section
7.4
.

Proposition 2.24
.

Under Assumption
2.19
,
for any
t\in\mathbb{T}
,
u(t,\cdot,\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}^{2,2}_{+,\alpha}
with
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
.

As a consequence of Propositions
2.13
and
2.14
, this yields the well-posedness of the PPDE, the main result of this section.

Theorem 2.25
.

Let Assumption
2.19
hold, then
u\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2,2}_{+,\alpha}
, with
\alpha=\frac{1}{2}
, and is the unique classical solution of the PPDE

(2.33)

\partial_{t}u+\zeta\psi(t,\omega_{t})^{2}\partial_{x}u+\frac{1}{2}\psi(t,\omega_{t})^{2}\partial_{xx}u+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\omega\omega}u,(K^{t},K^{t})\rangle+\rho\psi(t,\omega_{t})\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}u),K^{t}\rangle=0,

for all
(t,x,\omega)\in\overline{\Lambda}
and with boundary condition
u(T,x,\omega)=\phi(x)
.

Proof.

For a two-dimensional path
\bm{\omega}=(\omega^{(1)},\omega^{(2)})
, recall that
U(t,\bm{\omega})=u(t,\omega^{(1)}_{t},\omega^{(2)}\lvert_{[t,T]})
as defined in (
2.25
).
Let us start by checking that all the necessary assumptions are satisfied, namely
2.1
and
2.6
, the existence and uniqueness of strong solutions for our SDEs and
2.12
.
The two-dimensional SVE (
2.22
) is explicit and Lemma
2.21
provides the moment bounds hence Assumption
2.1
holds.
Regarding the coefficients,

\left\lvert\psi(y)\right\rvert\leq G(y)
,
\left\lvert\psi^{\prime}(y)\right\rvert\leq G(y)
,
\left\lvert\psi^{2}(y)\right\rvert\leq G^{2}(y)
and
\left\lvert(\psi^{2})^{\prime}(y)\right\rvert=\left\lvert 2\psi(y)\psi^{\prime}(y)\right\rvert\leq 2G^{2}(y)
for some
G

(and consequently
G^{2}
) in
\mathcal{X}
, from Assumption
2.19
(ii) and Lemma
2.21
, while
K
verifies Assumption
2.19
(iii) which ensures that Assumption
2.6
is satisfied.

Moreover, Assumption
2.12
(i) is also verified because of Lemma
2.21
and Assumption
2.19
, whereas Assumption
2.12
(ii) and (iii) are trivially satisfied as
f\equiv 0
.

The last ingredient is Proposition
2.24
, which allows us to apply Propositions
2.13
and
2.14
.
These propositions state that
U
is the unique solution to the PPDE (
2.10
)

\partial_{t}U+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U,(\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}},\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}})\rangle+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U,b^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle=0,

with terminal condition
U(T,\bm{\omega})=\phi(\omega^{(1)}_{T})
. Since
U(t,\bm{\omega})=u(t,\omega^{(1)}_{t},\omega^{(2)})
, the derivative in
\omega^{(1)}
is a standard derivative.
Thanks to Remark
2.11
and the definitions of the coefficients (
2.23
), the derivatives correspond to

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U,b^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\partial_{\omega^{(1)}}U,\zeta\psi(t,\omega_{t}^{(2)})^{2}\rangle=\zeta\psi(t,\omega_{t}^{(2)})^{2}\partial_{x}u,

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U,(\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}},\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\partial_{\omega^{(1)}\omega^{(1)}}U,(\psi(t,\omega^{(2)}_{t}),\psi_{t}(\omega^{(2)}_{t}))\rangle+2\langle\partial_{\omega^{(1)}\omega^{(2)}}U,(\rho\psi(t,\omega^{(2)}_{t}),K^{t})\rangle

\displaystyle\qquad+\langle\partial_{\omega^{(2)}\omega^{(2)}}U,(K^{t},K^{t})\rangle

\displaystyle=\psi(t,\omega^{(2)}_{t})^{2}\partial_{xx}u+2\rho\psi(t,\omega^{(2)}_{t})\langle\partial_{\omega^{(2)}}(\partial_{x}u),K^{t}\rangle+\langle\partial_{\omega^{(2)}\omega^{(2)}}u,(K^{t},K^{t})\rangle.

This boils down to (
2.33
).
∎

In passing, we showed that the functional Itô formula (
2.6
) holds for
u_{t}=u(t,X_{t},\Theta^{t})
:

Corollary 2.26
.

Let Assumption
2.19
hold, then the functional Itô formula holds

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u_{t}

\displaystyle=\Big\{\partial_{t}u_{t}+\zeta\psi(t,V_{t})^{2}\partial_{x}u_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\psi(t,V_{t})^{2}\partial_{xx}u_{t}+\rho\psi(t,V_{t})\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}u_{t}),K^{t}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\omega\omega}u_{t},(K^{t},K^{t})\rangle\Big\}\mathrm{d}t

(2.34)

\displaystyle\qquad+\psi(t,V_{t})\partial_{x}u_{t}\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\langle\partial_{\omega}u_{t},K^{t}\rangle\mathrm{d}W_{t}.

Proof.

Notice that
u\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2,2}_{+,\alpha}(\Gamma)
entails that
U\in\mathcal{C}^{1,2}_{+,\alpha}(\Lambda)
.
Recall that
u_{t}=u(t,X_{t},\Theta^{t})=U(t,\bm{X}\otimes_{t}\bm{\Theta}^{t})=U_{t}
. It thus suffices to apply the functional Itô formula (
2.6
) to
U_{t}
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}u_{t}=\mathrm{d}U_{t}

\displaystyle=\Big(\partial_{t}U_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}\bm{\omega}}U_{t},(\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}},\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}})\rangle+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U_{t},b^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle\Big)\mathrm{d}t+\langle\partial_{\bm{\omega}}U_{t},\sigma^{t,\bm{\omega}}\rangle\mathrm{d}\bm{W}_{t},

and identifying the derivatives as in the proof of Theorem
2.25
concludes the proof.
∎

3.
Weak rates of convergence for rough volatility

3.1.
The main result

Let
N\in\mathbb{N}
, set
\Delta:=\frac{T}{N}
. and
t_{i}:=i\Delta
for
i=0,\ldots,N
.
For conciseness we consider the model (
2.22
) with
\zeta=0
and
\psi(s,v)=\psi(v)
.
Relaxing the latter is only cumbersome but should not alter the main results while relaxing the former generates additional terms which require a more involved analysis. In the literature, the drift has always been considered uninfluential on the rate. As a matter of fact, the rate obtained in Case 1 of our main theorem does not hold if a drift is present (
\zeta\neq 0
).
We perform an Euler discretisation of
X
that we name
\overline{X}
:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\overline{X}_{t_{0}}=x_{0},

(3.2)

\displaystyle\overline{X}_{t_{i+1}}=\overline{X}_{t_{i}}+\psi(V_{t_{i}})(B_{t_{i+1}}-B_{t_{i}}),

for
i=0,\ldots,N-1
, where
V
is simulated exactly (e.g. by Cholesky decomposition).
Then, we extend to the whole time interval the process
\overline{X}
by interpolation

(3.3)

\displaystyle\overline{X}_{t}=x_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\psi(V_{\kappa_{s}})\mathrm{d}B_{s},

with
\kappa_{s}=t_{i}
for
s\in[t_{i},t_{i+1})
.
Our goal is to estimate the difference between the price and its approximation with respect to the number of grid points
N
:

\mathcal{E}^{N}:=\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T})]-\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(\overline{X}_{T}\right)\right].

From now on,
f(\Delta)\lesssim g(\Delta)
means there exists
C>0
such that
f(\Delta)\leq Cg(\Delta)
where
C
does not depend on
\Delta
(or equivalently on
N
), but may depend on other constants such as
p,H
and the growth constants of
\phi
and
\psi
.
Our main result characterises the weak rate of convergence of this numerical scheme as follows:

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
H\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
and
K(t,s)=(t-s)^{H-\frac{1}{2}}
for
s<t
and zero otherwise.

Case 1.

If
\phi
is quadratic and
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{{\rm exp}}(\mathbb{R})
then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{E}^{N}\right\rvert\lesssim\Delta
.

Case 2.

If
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{5}_{{\rm poly}}(\mathbb{R})
and
\psi\in\mathcal{C}^{3}_{{\rm exp}}(\mathbb{R})

then

\displaystyle\left\lvert\mathcal{E}^{N}\right\rvert\lesssim\bigstar(\Delta)
, where

\displaystyle\bigstar(\Delta):=\Delta^{3H+\frac{1}{2}}\mathbbm{1}_{H<\frac{1}{6}}+\Delta\mathbbm{1}_{H>\frac{1}{6}}+\Delta|\log(\Delta)|\mathbbm{1}_{H=\frac{1}{6}}.

Remark 3.2
.

The rate
\bigstar(\Delta)
was proven to be optimal in
[
14
, Proposition 6.1]
.

Remark 3.3
.

The regularity required for
\phi
and
\psi
is independent of
H
but depends on the number of Malliavin integration by parts required to disentangle the different terms.

Remark 3.4
.

The growth conditions and Lemma
2.21
imply that there exist
\bm{\mathrm{C}}>0
(depending only on the growth constants
\kappa_{\phi},\kappa_{\psi}
) such that for all
p\geq 1
and
k\leq 3,\,n\leq 5
,

(3.4)

\mathbb{E}\left[\Big\lvert\phi^{(n)}(X_{T})\Big\lvert^{p}\right]\leq\bm{\mathrm{C}}^{p},\quad\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big\lvert\psi_{t}^{(k)}(V_{t})\Big\lvert^{p}\right]\leq\bm{\mathrm{C}}^{p},\quad\sup_{t\in\mathbb{T}}\mathbb{E}\left[\Big\lvert\psi_{t}^{2(k)}(V_{t})\Big\lvert^{p}\right]\leq\bm{\mathrm{C}}^{p}.

3.2.
Decomposition of the error

We first decompose the error
in the spirit of
[
5
, Section 3.3]
. Let
\overline{u}_{t}:=u(t,\overline{X}_{t},\Theta^{t}_{[t,T]})
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
, then (
2.20
) yields
\overline{u}_{t}=\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T}^{t,\overline{X}_{t}})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{T})\lvert X_{t}=\overline{X}_{t},\Theta^{t}]
, where
X_{T}^{t,\overline{X}_{t}}:=\overline{X}_{t}+\int_{t}^{T}\psi(V_{s})\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Under Assumption
2.19
, we have

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\mathfrak{A}_{i},\qquad\text{where }\quad

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{i}:=\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\Big(\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{B}_{1}(t)+\rho\mathfrak{B}_{2}(t)\Big)\mathrm{d}t\qquad\text{for all }i\leq N-1,

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{1}(t):=\mathbb{E}\big[\left(\psi(V_{t})^{2}-\psi(V_{t_{i}})^{2}\right)\partial_{xx}\overline{u}_{t}\big],\qquad\mathfrak{B}_{2}(t):=\mathbb{E}\big[\big(\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})\big)\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle\big].

Remark 3.6
.

By virtue of Proposition
2.22
, the following representations hold:

(3.6)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}\partial_{xx}\overline{u}_{t}&=\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{\prime\prime}(X_{T}^{t,\overline{X}_{t}})|\,\mathcal{F}_{t}\right],\\
\left\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\right\rangle&=\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi^{\prime\prime}(X_{T}^{t,\overline{X}_{t}})\int_{t}^{T}\psi^{\prime}(V_{s})K(s,t)\,\mathrm{d}B_{s}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{t}\right].\end{array}\right.

Proof.

Recalling that
t_{0}=0
and
t_{N}=T
, we write
\mathcal{E}^{N}
as a telescopic sum:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(X_{T})-\phi\left(\overline{X}_{T}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\phi(X_{T})|X_{0}=\overline{X}_{0}\right]\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\left(\overline{X}_{T}\right)|X_{T}=\overline{X}_{T}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[u\left(t_{0},\overline{X}_{t_{0}},\Theta^{t_{0}}_{\left[t_{0},t_{N}\right]}\right)\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[u\left(t_{n},\overline{X}_{t_{N}},\Theta^{t_{N}}_{t_{N}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\Big\{\mathbb{E}\Big[u\left(t_{i},\overline{X}_{t_{i}},\Theta^{t_{i}}_{\left[t_{i},t_{N}\right]}\right)\Big]-\mathbb{E}\Big[u\left(t_{i+1},\overline{X}_{t_{i+1}},\Theta^{t_{i+1}}_{\left[t_{i+1},t_{N}\right]}\right)\Big]\Big\}

\displaystyle=:\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\mathfrak{A}_{i}.

We now show that the terms
(\mathfrak{A}_{i})_{i=0,\dots,N-1}
have the form given in the proposition.
By virtue of the representation (
3.3
) and the regularity of
u
from Proposition (
2.25
), we apply the functional Itô formula (
2.6
) on
[t_{i},t_{i+1})
to
u(t,\overline{X}_{t},\Theta^{t})

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\overline{u}_{t}=

\displaystyle\bigg(\partial_{t}\overline{u}_{t}+\psi(V_{t_{i}})^{2}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{xx}\overline{u}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\omega\omega}\overline{u}_{t},(K^{t},K^{t})\rangle+\rho\psi(V_{t_{i}})\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle\bigg)\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle+\psi(V_{t_{i}})\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}+\langle\partial_{\omega}\overline{u}_{t},K^{t}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}.

Combining this with the path-dependent PDE (
2.33
) with
\zeta=0
, we obtain, as claimed,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[u\left(t_{i},\overline{X}_{t_{i}},\Theta^{t_{i}}_{[t_{i},T]}\right)-u\left(t_{i+1},\overline{X}_{t_{i+1}},\Theta^{t_{i+1}}_{[t_{i+1},T]}\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left\{\partial_{t}\overline{u}_{t}+\psi(V_{t_{i}})^{2}\frac{1}{2}\partial_{xx}\overline{u}_{t}+\frac{1}{2}\langle\partial_{\omega\omega}\overline{u}_{t},(K^{t},K^{t})\rangle+\rho\psi(V_{t_{i}})\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle\right\}\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left\{\left(\psi(V_{t})^{2}-\psi(V_{t_{i}})^{2}\right)\frac{1}{2}\partial_{xx}\overline{u}_{t}+\rho\Big(\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})\Big)\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle\right\}\mathrm{d}t\right].

∎

3.3.
Proof of Theorem
3.1
-Case 1

We start with a technical lemma
[
14
, Lemma 2.2]
:

Lemma 3.7
.

Consider the function
\varphi:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\varphi(t):=\mathbb{E}\left[g\left(V_{t}\right)\right],

where
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a Schwartz function.
Then
\varphi
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
and, for any
0\leq u\leq t
,

\left|\varphi(t)-\varphi(u)\right|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup_{s\in\mathbb{T}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert g^{\prime\prime}(V_{s})\right\rvert\right]\left(t^{2H}-u^{2H}\right).

In cases where the (local) weak error rate of the integrand in
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
is independent of
t
, say
f(\Delta)
, then the global rate is
\left\lvert\mathcal{E}^{N}\right\rvert\lesssim\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\mathrm{d}tf(\Delta)=f(\Delta)
. Thanks to the following observation, we obtain rate one even when the weak local error
t^{2H}-t_{i}^{2H}
in Lemma
3.7
would yield a lower rate.

Lemma 3.8
.

For any
\gamma>0
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}(t^{\gamma}-t_{i}^{\gamma})\mathrm{d}t\leq T^{1+\gamma}\Delta
.

Proof.

The following computations are straightforward:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}(t^{\gamma}-t_{i}^{\gamma})\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\left\{\frac{t_{i+1}^{\gamma+1}-t_{i}^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma+1}-t_{i}^{\gamma}(t_{i+1}-t_{i})\right\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\left\{\frac{((i+1)\Delta)^{\gamma+1}-(i\Delta)^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma+1}-(i\Delta)^{\gamma}\Delta\right\}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{\gamma+1}\left\{\frac{1}{\gamma+1}\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\left[(i+1)^{\gamma+1}-i^{\gamma+1}\right]-\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}i^{\gamma}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\Delta^{\gamma+1}\left\{\frac{N^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma+1}-\int_{0}^{N-1}x^{\gamma}\mathrm{d}x\right\}=\frac{\Delta^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma+1}\left(N^{\gamma+1}-(N-1)^{\gamma+1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\Delta^{\gamma+1}}{\gamma+1}\max_{x\in[N-1,N]}x^{\gamma}(\gamma+1)\left(N-(N-1)\right)=\Delta^{\gamma+1}N^{\gamma}=\Delta T^{\gamma+1},

where the last line follows from the mean value theorem and the monotonicity of
x\mapsto x^{\gamma}
.
∎

Case 1 of Theorem
3.1
(with quadratic payoff) follows immediately by showing that, for each
i\leq N
,
\left\lvert\mathfrak{A}_{i}\right\rvert\lesssim\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\big(t^{2H}-t_{i}^{2H}\big)\mathrm{d}t
and invoking Lemma
3.8
.

We start with applying the Clark-Ocone formula to
\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})
, which is possible since
\psi
is differentiable and
V_{t}\in\mathbb{D}^{1,2}
:

\displaystyle\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})]+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{D}^{W}_{s}\mathbb{E}[\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{s}]\mathrm{d}W_{s}.

Integration by parts then yields

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{2}(t)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})]\mathbb{E}[\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{D}^{W}_{s}\mathbb{E}[\psi(V_{t})-\psi(V_{t_{i}})\lvert\mathcal{F}_{s}]\mathrm{D}^{W}_{s}\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle\mathrm{d}s\right].

Note that for
t\geq 0
, if
\phi
is quadratic then
\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle=0
as can be seen from (
3.6
).
Since
\mathbb{E}[\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle]
is bounded we get
\left\lvert\mathfrak{B}_{2}(t)\right\rvert\lesssim t^{2H}-t_{i}^{2H}

by Lemma
3.7
. The same strategy applies to
\mathfrak{B}_{1}
, which yields the claim.

Remark 3.9
.

If a drift is present (
\zeta\neq 0
), then
\mathrm{D}_{s}\langle\partial_{\omega}(\partial_{x}\overline{u}_{t}),K^{t}\rangle
may not be null for a quadratic
\phi
and the conclusion may not hold.

3.4.
Proof of Theorem
3.1
-Case 2

The main computational ingredients are decomposed in two parts, whose proofs are postponed to Sections
5
and
6
respectively, relying on independent estimates developed in Section
4
.

Proposition 3.10
.

For any
t\in[t_{i},t_{i+1})
,

\left\lvert\mathfrak{B}_{1}(t)\right\rvert\lesssim\bigstar(\Delta)+(t^{2H}-t_{i}^{2H})
.

Proposition 3.11
.

For any
t\in[t_{i},t_{i+1})
,

\left\lvert\mathfrak{B}_{2}(t)\right\rvert\lesssim\bigstar(\Delta)+(t^{2H}-t_{i}^{2H})
.

This is sufficient to finish the proof of the main result invoking Lemma
3.8
:

\mathcal{E}^{N}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\mathfrak{A}_{i}=\sum_{i=0}^{N-1}\int_{t_{i}}^{t_{i+1}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{B}_{1}(t)+\mathfrak{B}_{2}(t)\right)\mathrm{d}t\lesssim\bigstar(\Delta).

4.
Useful results

To prove Propositions
3.10
and
3.11
in Sections
5
and
6
, we require a few estimates of independent interests,
which we gather in this section.
Let us introduce the following useful notations:

•

As before, for all
r\in\mathbb{T}
,
\kappa(r)=\max\{t_{j},\,t_{j}\leq r\}
.
Furthermore, for all
r\in\mathbb{T}
,
define
\tau(r)
as the unique integer such that
t_{\tau(r)-1}=\kappa_{r}
(equivalently
t_{\tau(r)}=\kappa_{r}+\Delta
).
In particular,

t_{\tau(r)-1}=\kappa_{r}\leq r<\kappa_{r}+\Delta=t_{\tau(r)}
.

•

We write
A\lesssim B
whenever there is a constant
c>0
such that
A\lesssim cB
and
c
only depends on
H
and
L^{p}
bounds of
\phi,\,\psi
and their derivatives (finite thanks to Assumption
2.19
).

Instead of the crude Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we rely extensively on the Malliavin integration by parts

\mathbb{E}[Y\int Z_{s}\mathrm{d}W_{s}]=\mathbb{E}[\int\mathrm{D}^{W}_{s}YZ_{s}\mathrm{d}s]
:
this is necessary to achieve the optimal rate but induces a higher number of terms to estimate.
Combining the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, the
L^{p}
bounds of
\phi,\psi
(and their derivatives) and Lemmas
4.1
and
4.2
, the quantities of interest can all nevertheless be reduced to deterministic integrals.
Then, we leverage Lemmas
4.3
to
4.8
and analogous computational tricks for fractional integrals to obtain the desired bounds.
In particular, when direct computation is not feasible, we regularly decompose the integrals on the partition
(t_{j})_{j=0}^{i}
and then replace the running variables by the closest point on that grid, e.g. for
s\in[t_{j},t_{j+1}]
and
k\leq j-1
, we have
(s-t_{k})^{\alpha}\leq(t_{j}-t_{k})^{\alpha}=(j-k)^{\alpha}\Delta^{\alpha}
.
Finally, the last step consists in estimating the sums.

The following results are used on several occasions.
First of them is the following trivial inequality, stated without proof, which will appear in many forms repeatedly: for any continuous function
g
decreasing on some interval
[a,b]
,
with
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
, then

(4.1)

\sum_{u=a}^{b}g(u)\lesssim\sum_{\ell=a}^{b}\int_{\ell-1}^{\ell}g(u)\mathrm{d}u=\int_{a-1}^{b}g(u)\mathrm{d}u.

4.1.
Stochastic estimates

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
Y
be an
L^{2}(\Omega)
-random variable and
Z
be an adapted process such that
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{s\in\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert Z(s)\right\rvert]
is finite.
Then, for all
0\leq r\leq t\leq t_{i+1}
:

(4.2)

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert\int_{r}^{t}\left\lvert Z(s)\right\rvert K(\kappa_{s},r)\mathrm{d}B_{s}\right]\lesssim\Big(1+K(\kappa_{r}+\Delta,r)\sqrt{\Delta}\Big)\mathbbm{1}_{r<t_{i}}.

Proof.

By Cauchy-Schwarz inequality and Itô’s isometry,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\lvert Y\right\rvert\int_{r}^{t}\left\lvert Z(s)\right\rvert K(\kappa_{s},r)\mathrm{d}B_{s}\right]^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[Y^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{s\in\mathbb{T}}\left\lvert Z(s)\right\rvert^{2}\right]\int_{r}^{t}K(\kappa_{s},r)^{2}\mathrm{d}s.

Notice that
K(\kappa_{r},r)=0
so that, for all
r\in[0,t)
, it holds
\int_{r}^{\kappa_{r}+\Delta}K(\kappa_{s},r)^{2}\mathrm{d}s=0
. In particular, for
r\in[0,t_{i})
, we have
\int_{r}^{t}K(\kappa_{s},r)^{2}\mathrm{d}s=0
.
For
r<t_{i}
, we write

\displaystyle\left|\int_{r}^{t}K(\kappa_{s},r)^{2}\mathrm{d}s\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{j=\tau(r)}^{i-1}K(t_{j},r)^{2}\Delta+K(t_{i},r)^{2}(t-t_{i})\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq K(t_{\tau(r)},r)\sqrt{\Delta}+\left|\sum_{j=\tau(r)+1}^{i}K(t_{j},r)^{2}\Delta\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq K(\kappa_{r}+\Delta,r)\sqrt{\Delta}+\left|\sum_{j=\tau(r)+1}^{i}\int_{t_{j-1}}^{t_{j}}K(t_{j},r)^{2}\mathrm{d}u\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=K(\kappa_{r}+\Delta,r)\sqrt{\Delta}+\left|\int_{t_{\tau(r)}}^{t_{i}}K(u,r)^{2}\mathrm{d}u\right|^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=K(\kappa_{r}+\Delta,r)\sqrt{\Delta}+\frac{(t_{i}-t_{\tau(r)})^{H}}{\sqrt{2H}},

and the claim follows.
∎

The following lemma is a conditional version of
[
14
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Lemma 4.2
.

For all
r\leq t
and
g\in\mathcal{C}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
define

G_{r}(t):=\mathbb{E}_{r}[g(V_{t})]
. For all
r\leq t_{i}
,

G_{r}(t)-G_{r}(t_{i})=\int_{t_{i}}^{t}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{r}[g^{\prime}(V_{t})]\left(H-\frac{1}{2}\right)\int_{0}^{r}(s-u)^{H-\frac{3}{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{u}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{E}_{r}[g^{\prime\prime}(V_{t})](s-r)^{2H-1}\right\}\mathrm{d}s.

This leads to the estimate

\left\lvert G_{r}(t)-G_{r}(t_{i})\right\rvert\lesssim\sup_{q\in[0,t],n=1,2}\left\lvert\mathbb{E}_{r}[g^{(n)}(V_{q})]\right\rvert\int_{t_{i}}^{t}\left\{\left\lvert\int_{0}^{r}(s-u)^{H-\frac{3}{2}}\mathrm{d}W_{u}\right\rvert+(s-r)^{2H-1}\right\}\mathrm{d}s.

Proof.

Assume first that
g
is a Schwartz function, in which case the proof mimics that of
[
14
, Lemma 2.1]
but in a conditional fashion.
Indeed, in this case
g
admits a Fourier transform, which reads
\widehat{g}(\xi):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}\xi x}g(x)\mathrm{d}x
, for all
\xi\in\mathbb{R}.

Since the Fourier transform preserves the Schwartz property, then
g
